--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Let me analyze the mission carefully:

**Before switch time:** Score increases by 1 for every robot that is **stationary** (bad to be still)
**After switch time:** Score increases by 1 for every robot that is **moving** (bad to move)

So the optimal strategy is:
- **Before switch**: All robots should be **moving**
- **After switch**: All robots should be **stopped**

The switch is signaled by a **GREEN wall block lighting up**. Robots can detect colored objects with their camera (condition C=7, color detection).

Green corresponds to color index 2 in the color set {1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan}.

**Ideal FSM:**
- State 0: Moving (Exploration or similar) 
- State 1: Stop
- Transition from State 0 → State 1: When robot perceives GREEN color (C=7, l=2)
- Transition from State 1 → State 0: Maybe never, or very low probability

The champion is `--nstates 1 --s0 1` (always stop), scoring 10095. This is bad because before the switch, robots should be moving.

A 2-state machine where robots start moving and switch to stop upon seeing green should be much better.

Let me design:
- State 0: Exploration (moving) → transition to Stop when sees green
- State 1: Stop → stay there (or very low probability transition back)

The switch time is 400-600 steps out of 1200. So roughly 400-600 steps moving (bad) + 600-800 steps stopped (good). With always-stop: ~400-600 steps moving score saved but ~600-800 steps of stopped penalty before switch... wait.

Before switch: stationary is bad. Always-stop gets ~500 steps × 20 robots = 10000 penalty before switch, then 0 after. Total ~10000. That matches the champion's score of ~10095.

If robots always move: 0 before switch, ~700×20 = 14000 after switch. Worse.

If robots start moving and stop when they see green: ~0 before switch (good!), ~0 after switch (good!). This should score very low!

The key is the green detection. Color 2 = green. Robots near the lit wall will see it and stop, then spread the info... but only robots near the wall see it directly. Others need neighbor-based signals.

Let me design several FSMs:

1. **Best guess**: 2-state: Explore → Stop on seeing green (C=7, l=2)
2. Variation: Add attraction to spread green-seeing robots' positions
3. Use LED colors to propagate signal (robots seeing green change LED, others detect that LED color)

FSM: --nstates 1 --s0 1 --cle0 6 (champion, copy verbatim)

Now let me build the 14 machines:

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 10 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 1.0 --s1 1 --cle1 6 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.01

And variations. Let me think about which LED colors to use for signaling. If robots seeing green emit a specific LED color (say cyan=6), others could detect cyan and also stop. But C=7 detects object colors from the camera - does it detect robot LEDs? Yes, the range-and-bearing camera can see colored LEDs on other robots.

So: State 0 (Explore, LED off) → see green → State 1 (Stop, LED cyan)
State 0 also: see cyan LED → stop

This creates a cascade effect!

Let me design carefully:

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 10 --cle0 0 --n0 2 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 1.0 --n0x1 0 --c0x1 7 --l0x1 6 --p0x1 1.0 --s1 1 --cle1 6 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.01

This is promising. Let me create 14 diverse machines.

Wait, I need to be careful with the transition syntax. Let me re-read...

`--n<I>x<K> D` : destination indexed among OTHER states. With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0. With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0,2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.

So for 2 states: from state 0, D=0 means go to state 1. From state 1, D=0 means go to state 0.
For 3 states: from state 0, others are [1,2], D=0→state1, D=1→state2. From state 1, others are [0,2], D=0→state0, D=1→state2. From state 2, others are [0,1], D=0→state0, D=1→state1.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion scores 1246.39 with a 2-state machine:
- State 0: Repulsion (spread out, LED off) → transition to State 1 when seeing green (color 2) OR cyan (color 6, which is the LED of stopped robots)
- State 1: Stop (LED cyan) → tiny prob (0.005) back to State 0

This is excellent! The cascade works: robots spread out (repulsion), when they see green wall they stop and light cyan, nearby robots see cyan and also stop. Score dropped from ~10095 to ~1246.

Now I need to exploit and explore around this champion.

**Key parameters to vary:**
- Repulsion gain (currently 2.0)
- LED color of stopped state (currently cyan=6)
- The cascade LED color to detect (currently 6=cyan)
- Transition probability back to state 0 (currently 0.005)
- Number of transitions from state 1
- Add more transitions for robustness

**Exploitation ideas:**
1. Try different rep gains (1.0, 1.5, 2.5, 3.0)
2. Try different back-transition probabilities (0.001, 0.002, 0.01)
3. Add a third transition from state 0 (e.g., neighbour count)
4. Try exploration instead of repulsion in state 0

**Exploration ideas:**
1. 3-state machines with intermediate cascade state
2. Use attraction in state 0 before switch (robots cluster near walls more)
3. Try GoToColor green approach
4. Repulsion with different LED colors for cascade

The champion uses repulsion which spreads robots across the arena, maximizing coverage so more robots see the green wall. The cyan LED cascade propagates the stop signal.

Let me be careful with the format:
- `--n<I>x<K> D` needs destination D
- `--c<I>x<K> C` needs condition type
- For C=7: needs `--l<I>x<K>` and `--p<I>x<K>`
- For C=5: needs `--p<I>x<K>`

From state 0 (2 states): others=[1], so D must be 0
From state 1 (2 states): others=[0], so D must be 0

From state 0 (3 states): others=[1,2], D=0→state1, D=1→state2
From state 1 (3 states): others=[0,2], D=0→state0, D=1→state2
From state 2 (3 states): others=[0,1], D=0→state0, D=1→state1

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 5 --rep0 2 --cle0 0 --n0 2 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --p0x0 1 --l0x0 2 --n0x1 0 --c0x1 7 --p0x1 1 --l0x1 6 --s1 1 --cle1 6 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.005

Now variations:

1. Rep gain 1.5
2. Rep gain 2.5
3. Rep gain 3.0
4. Rep gain 1.0
5. Back prob 0.001
6. Back prob 0.002
7. Back prob 0.01
8. Add third transition from state 0 (probabilistic fallback)
9. 3-state machine
10. Exploration instead of repulsion
11. GoToColor green
12. Different cascade LED (magenta=5)
13. Rep gain 2, no back transition (p=0.0001)

## Reasoning

The champion scores 1133.37 with:
- State 0: Repulsion (rep=2, LED off) → see green (color 2) → State 1; see cyan (color 6) → State 1
- State 1: Stop (LED cyan) → prob 0.0001 → State 0

The very low back-transition probability (0.0001) won the round - robots almost never return to moving once stopped. This makes sense: once the green signal is detected, stay stopped.

**Key insights:**
- The cascade (cyan LED) works well for propagating the stop signal
- Very low back-transition probability is better (0.0001 beat 0.001, 0.002, 0.005)
- Repulsion spreads robots to cover the arena for green detection
- Maybe even 0 back-transition (but we need --n1 with at least 1 transition... or do we? Let me check: minimum transitions is 1 per state)

**Exploitation:**
1. Try even lower back prob: 0.00005, 0.00001
2. Try rep gains: 1.5, 1.0, 2.5, 3.0
3. Try adding a 3rd transition from state 0 (neighbor count threshold)
4. Try 3-state with better cascade

**Exploration:**
1. Can state 1 have 0 transitions? No, minimum is 1. But what if back-prob is extremely tiny like 0.00001?
2. Try different LED colors for cascade
3. 3-state machines

Let me be very careful about format. The errors say I'm missing `--n0`, destination indices, `--n1`, `--cle0`. Let me write very explicitly.

Format reminder:
- `--n<I> T` declares number of transitions from state I
- `--n<I>x<K> D` declares destination of transition K from state I (D is index among OTHER states)
- `--c<I>x<K> C` declares condition type
- For 2 states: from state 0, D=0 → state 1; from state 1, D=0 → state 0

- Higher repulsion gain is better (1.0 → 2.0 → 4.0, all improving)
- Very low back-transition probability is better (0.0001)
- Cyan LED cascade works well

1. 3-state machines with rep=4 or 5

The pattern is clear: maximize repulsion to spread robots across arena (better green wall coverage), minimize back-transition probability (once stopped, stay stopped). Let me push rep to the max (5.0) and try extremely low back probs.

I must be extremely careful about format - the errors keep appearing. Let me write each machine with explicit attention to every required parameter.

Format checklist for each state I:
- `--s<I> B [params]` ✓
- `--cle<I> L` ✓
- `--n<I> T` ✓
- For each transition K: `--n<I>x<K> D` `--c<I>x<K> C` [condition params] ✓

For 2-state machines:
- From state 0: `--n0x<K> 0` (destination is always 0, meaning state 1)
- From state 1: `--n1x<K> 0` (destination is always 0, meaning state 0)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion is still 1017.70 with rep=4, back-prob=0.0001. Round 5 didn't improve it, meaning rep=5, rep=4.5 etc. didn't beat rep=4. So rep=4 seems near-optimal for repulsion gain.

The score of ~1017 still has room to improve. Let me think about what's causing residual penalty:
- Some robots may not detect the green signal in time (limited camera range)
- The cascade via cyan LED may not propagate fast enough
- Some robots may remain in repulsion state too long after switch

**What could further reduce score:**
1. Better signal propagation - maybe add more cascade colors
2. Robots that don't see green/cyan still accumulate post-switch moving penalty
3. Maybe a 3-state machine where state 2 is also stop but with different LED to extend cascade reach

**I keep getting format errors** - let me be even more explicit. The errors say state 0 is missing `--n0`, transition missing destination, state 1 missing `--n1`. This is very strange because I've been including these. Let me check the 3-state machine format very carefully.

For 3-state machine, state 1: `--n1 1 --n1x0 0` - destination D=0 means "first among others from state 1" = state 0. That's correct.

Wait - could the issue be with my 3-state machine where I wrote `--n1x0 0` but meant to go to state 0 (which IS index 0 among others [0,2])? That should be fine.

Let me focus on 2-state machines mostly since they work, and be very careful with any 3-state machines.

**Strategy for round 6:**
- Keep champion (rep=4, back=0.0001, cyan cascade)
- Exploit: try slightly different rep values (3.8, 4.2), different back probs
- Try adding a 4th transition type or different cascade mechanism
- Exploration: try if state 1 (stop) also reacts to seeing green (to reinforce staying stopped) - but it can't transition to itself...
- Try: state 1 with 2 transitions - one very low prob back to state 0, one detecting absence of green going somewhere... no.
- Try: 3-state where state 0=repulsion, state 1=stop+cyan, state 2=stop+magenta; cascade from green→state1, from cyan→state2, from magenta→state2. This extends cascade range.

For 3-state machines, destinations from state 0: D=0→state1, D=1→state2.

## Reasoning

The champion is stuck at 1017.70 for 3 rounds now. The core design (rep=4, cyan cascade, back-prob=0.0001) seems solid but isn't improving. Let me think more carefully about what's limiting performance.

**Analysis of residual score ~1017:**
- 20 robots, 1200 steps total
- Switch at ~500 steps (average)
- Before switch (~500 steps): robots moving = 0 penalty. Good.
- After switch (~700 steps): robots that haven't stopped yet = penalty
- Score ~1017 / 20 robots / 700 steps ≈ 0.07 → about 7% of robots still moving after switch on average

The bottleneck is: robots that can't see green or cyan quickly enough after the switch. The cascade propagates at the speed of robot proximity (0.5m range). With repulsion spreading robots apart, the cascade might be slow to reach all robots.

**New ideas:**
1. Maybe repulsion is TOO strong - robots spread so far apart that the cyan cascade can't reach them (>0.5m apart). A moderate repulsion might give better cascade connectivity.
2. After stopping, robots emit cyan - but if they're far apart, the signal doesn't propagate. Maybe attraction after stopping would help? But state 1 is Stop (can't move).
3. Try: state 0 = repulsion, state 1 = stop with cyan, but also add transitions from state 0 based on neighbor count (if isolated = keep going, if has neighbors = stop to cascade).
4. Try a completely different approach: exploration (random walk) instead of repulsion, so robots stay closer together for better cascade.
5. Try: lower repulsion (rep=2 or 3) combined with very low back-prob.

Actually, the issue might be that with rep=4, robots push to walls and get stuck there, far from each other. The cascade then fails because inter-robot distances exceed 0.5m.

**Key insight to try:** Use rep=2 or rep=1 with back-prob=0.0001. This keeps robots more clustered, cascade propagates faster. Or use exploration (random walk) which keeps robots within moderate distances.

Let me also try: 3-state where state 2 is exploration (not repulsion) so after the cascade stops propagating, remaining robots in state 0 can wander closer to stopped robots.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Massive improvement! Score dropped from 1017.70 to 283.76 with GoToColor green (behavior 8) instead of repulsion!

**Why GoToColor green works so well:**
- Before switch: no green visible, so robots wander/move (avoiding stationary penalty)
- After switch: green wall lights up, robots move TOWARD it (still moving = bad after switch... wait)

Actually wait - after switch, robots moving toward green wall IS moving, which accumulates penalty. But the score is 283 which is much better than 1017. 

The key insight: GoToColor green makes robots move before the switch (good - avoids stationary penalty). When green appears, they move toward the wall, but quickly detect green AND cyan, and transition to Stop (state 1). The transition probability is 1.0 for seeing green, so they stop almost immediately when green appears.

**The winning design:**
- State 0: GoToColor green (vel=1.0, clr=2, LED off) → see green (p=1.0) → Stop; see cyan (p=1.0) → Stop
- State 1: Stop (LED cyan) → tiny prob (0.0001) → back to state 0

Before switch: no green visible, GoToColor wanders (moving = good, avoids pre-switch penalty)
After switch: immediately sees green wall, transitions to Stop with p=1.0

**Exploitation:**
1. Try different vel (but it must be 1.0 - fixed)
2. Try different back-prob: 0.00005, 0.00001, 0.0002
3. Try different cascade LED colors
4. Try 3 transitions from state 0 (add more cascade colors)
5. Try GoAwayColor instead of GoToColor (behavior 9)

**Exploration:**
1. 3-state machines with GoToColor
2. Try GoToColor with different clr values (does color 2 = green? Yes)
3. Try: state 0 = GoToColor green, but also detect other LEDs for cascade

Note: `--vel0` must be `1.0` (with decimal point), `--clr0` is the target color.

Let me write 13 machines carefully:

## Reasoning

New champion: GoAwayColor green (behavior 9) with score 264.29, beating GoToColor green (283.76)!

**Why GoAwayColor green works even better:**
- Before switch: no green visible, robots wander/move freely (avoiding stationary penalty)
- After switch: green wall lights up, robots move AWAY from it - but they also immediately detect green (p=1.0) and transition to Stop. So they stop almost instantly.
- The key difference from GoToColor: GoAwayColor may spread robots more evenly across the arena before the switch (moving away from walls), giving better coverage for the cascade.

**Current champion:**
- State 0: GoAwayColor green (vel=1.0, clr=2, LED off) → see green → Stop; see cyan → Stop
- State 1: Stop (LED cyan) → prob 0.0001 → State 0

**Exploitation targets:**
1. Back-prob variations: 0.00005, 0.00001, 0.0002, 0.0005
2. Add more cascade colors as transitions from state 0
3. Try different LED for stopped state (yellow=4, magenta=5) with matching cascade
4. Try 3 or 4 transitions from state 0

**Exploration:**
1. 3-state machines with GoAwayColor
2. GoAwayColor + different clr values
3. State 1 with 2 transitions (back + react to green again)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

New champion: score 252.66 with `--p1x0 0` (back-probability = 0, i.e., never return to state 0 from state 1). Once stopped, robots stay stopped forever. This makes perfect sense - once the green signal is detected, there's no reason to ever start moving again.

**Key insight:** The back-transition probability of 0 means state 1 is absorbing. But wait - condition C=5 with p=0 means it never fires. So state 1 has a declared transition that never triggers. This is the best possible behavior: stop and stay stopped.

**What's the residual score of ~252?**
- 20 robots, switch at ~500 steps average
- Before switch (~500 steps): robots in GoAwayColor state are MOVING (score=0 for those steps) ✓
- After switch (~700 steps): robots need to detect green/cyan and stop
- Some robots may not see green or cyan quickly, accumulating moving penalty
- Score ~252 / 20 robots ≈ 12.6 steps per robot of moving after switch

**What can still improve:**
1. Better cascade - more colors detected from state 0
2. The cyan cascade is key - robots that stop emit cyan, others detect it
3. Maybe add more cascade colors (yellow=4, magenta=5) to state 0 transitions
4. Try 3-state with intermediate cascade states
5. The champion detects green (2) and cyan (6) - adding more LED colors could help

**For this final round:**
- Keep champion exactly
- Exploit: add 3rd/4th cascade color transitions
- Try: state 1 emits different LED, state 0 detects that LED too
- Try 3-state with two stopping states emitting different LEDs for richer cascade
- Try state 0 detecting yellow (4) in addition

Note: `--p1x0 0` means probability 0 - the transition is declared but never fires. This is valid.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
